# Atlantic slave trade

This article is about historic trade practices involving human enslavement. For the modern trade involving these regions, see [Trans-Atlantic trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Atlantic_trade).

Reproduction of a handbill advertising a slave auction in [Charleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina), British [Province of South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_South_Carolina), in 1769

The **Atlantic slave trade** or **transatlantic slave trade** involved the transportation by slave traders of [enslaved African people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Africa) to the [Americas](29833.md). This trade was operated by [slave ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_ships) from both Europe and the [New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World). Some voyages used the [triangular trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangular_trade) route and its [Middle Passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Passage), particularly in the early phases.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEltis202547-1] Europeans established a coastal [slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery) in the 15th century, and trade to the Americas began in the 16th century,[^cite_note-4] lasting through the 19th century.[^cite_note-5] Most transported in the transatlantic trade were from [Central](539606.md) and [West Africa](67393.md). In contrast to other slave trades, those taking slaves from Africa generally bought their cargo from West and Central African slave merchants. This applied to Europeans in the transatlantic trade and Arab slave traders supplying their own markets. While Europeans were involved in some raiding, this was uncommon and discouraged.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEltis202541-6][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998112-7] The Portuguese built [barracoons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barracoon) in their African slaving bases to gather newly purchased captives ready for the next ship to arrive. Northern Europeans operated some forts[^cite_note-9] as African bases, originally for trading in gold, but later for slaves. However, the usual approach of North European slave traders was to use their ship as a base in which to gather their cargo. This meant these ships spent much longer on the African coast than Portuguese vessels, and the first slaves the slavers bought endured longer confinement on board.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEltis2025168-10]

The Americas had, after Australia, a much lower population density than other inhabited continents. Exploitation by the European conquerors of these lands needed a workforce, but the sparse indigenous population was shrinking due to European diseases. Demand for labour could not be met by Europeans, who were reluctant to emigrate at this early stage. The solution was the same used for the sugar plantations of [Madeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeira) and [Canary Islands](5717.md) c. 1450: slaves brought from Africa.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEltis2025xxiii,_4–7-11] Slaves were sold in the Americas to work on coffee, tobacco, cocoa, sugar, and cotton [plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation), gold and silver mines, rice fields, the construction industry, cutting timber for ships, as skilled labour, and as domestic servants.[^cite_note-Covey-Eisnach_2009-12] The first enslaved Africans sent to the [English colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_overseas_possessions) were classified as [indentured servants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indentured_servants), with legal standing similar to contract-based workers from Britain and Ireland. By the middle of the 17th century, slavery had hardened as a racial caste, with African slaves and their future offspring legally the property of their owners, as children born to slave mothers were also slaves (*[partus sequitur ventrem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partus_sequitur_ventrem)*). As property, the people were considered merchandise or units of labour, and [sold at markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_market) with other goods and services, their prices determined by their health and expected productivity.[^cite_note-Fogel1994-13][^cite_note-Berlin2012-14]

The major Atlantic slave trading nations, in order of trade volume, were [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire), [Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire), [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire), [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire), the [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Empire), the United States, and [Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_overseas_colonies).[^cite_note-Curtin1969-15] The largest slave trading ports were in South America, with New World-based ships probably carrying more enslaved people from Africa than those travelling in ships with a home port in Europe. The current estimate is that about 12.75 million Africans were loaded onto slave ships for transportation to the Americas. Of these, up to 2 million died during the voyage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEltis20252,_42,_45-16] About 8 million Africans were enslaved and remained on the African continent.[^cite_note-Manning1992-17][^cite_note-Davidson1980-18] Near the beginning of the 19th century, various governments acted to ban the trade, although illegal smuggling still occurred.

The Atlantic slave trade developed after trade contacts were established between the "[Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World)" ([Afro-Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Eurasia)) and "[New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World)" (the Americas). The [ocean currents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_current) and [prevailing winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prevailing_wind) of the Atlantic represented a barrier to return travel for seafarers who did not understand their pattern, whilst those with sufficient knowledge could use them as a tool to assist them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199815–17-19] In the 15th century, European developments in seafaring technologies produced multi-masted sailing vessels with centre-line rudders, replacing the medieval [cog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cog_\(ship\)) and the Mediterranean round ship. This led to vessels more suited to Atlantic seafaring.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams201369–70-20] Between 1600 and 1800, approximately 300,000 sailors engaged in the slave trade visited West Africa.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChristopher2006127-21] In doing so, they came into contact with societies along the West African coast and in the Americas which they had never previously encountered.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199813-22] [Pierre Chaunu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Chaunu) termed the consequences of European navigation *[désenclavement](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/d%C3%A9senclavement)*, with it marking an end of isolation for some societies and an increase in inter-societal contact in maritime regions.[^cite_note-Chaunu1969-23][^cite_note-24]

Historian [John Thornton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Thornton_\(historian\)) noted, "technical and geographical factors combined to make Europeans the most likely people to explore the Atlantic and develop its commerce".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199824-25] He identified these as being the drive to find profitable commercial opportunities outside Europe. There was the desire to create an alternative trade network to that controlled by the Muslim [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), which was viewed as a commercial, political and religious threat to European Christendom. European traders wanted to trade for gold, which could be found in West Africa, and find a maritime route to "the Indies" (India), where they could trade luxury goods such as spices, without having to obtain them from Islamic traders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199824–26-26]



Portuguese mariners used [caravel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravel) ships and traveled south along the West African coast and colonised [Cape Verde](18962637.md) in 1462.

[^cite_note-27]

European overseas expansion led to contact between the Old and New Worlds, producing the [Columbian exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_exchange) named after [Christopher Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus). It started the [global silver trade from the 16th to 18th centuries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_silver_trade_from_the_16th_to_18th_centuries), and involved the transfer of goods unique to one hemisphere to the other. Europeans brought cattle, horses, pigs, sheep, and chickens to the New World, and from the New World Europeans received turkeys, tobacco, potatoes, tomatoes, and maize.[^cite_note-Earle2012-28] Other commodities becoming important in trade were the sugarcane, coffee,[^cite_note-Earle2012-28] and cotton crops of the Americas,[^cite_note-Knight2012-29] along with their gold and silver.[^cite_note-Wiesner-Hanks2022-30]

During the [first wave of European colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_wave_of_European_colonization), although many Atlantic naval explorations were led by the [Iberian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) *[conquistadors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquistador)*, members of many European nationalities were involved, including from [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_Spain), [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Portugal), [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France), [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England), the [Italian states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_city-states), and the [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic). This diversity led Thornton to describe the "exploration of the Atlantic" as "a truly international exercise, even if many of the dramatic discoveries were made under the sponsorship of the Iberian monarchs". That gave rise to the myth that "the Iberians were the sole leaders of the exploration".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199827-31]

### European slavery in Portugal and Spain

See also: [Slavery in medieval Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_medieval_Europe), [Slavery in al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_al-Andalus), [Slavery in Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Portugal), [Slavery in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Spain), [Saqaliba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saqaliba), [Black Sea slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_slave_trade), and [Balkan slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_slave_trade)

Slavery had existed in Portugal and Spain throughout recorded history. The [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) had established [its system of slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_slavery) in ancient times. Benjamin Isaac posits a [proto-racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-racism) that existed among [Greco-Roman people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_world), referring to [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle)'s statement in his *[Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_\(Aristotle\))* that "slaves are subhuman or lesser men while masters are superior", to justify the inferior status of slaves.[^cite_note-Isaac2013-32] Since the [fall of the Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire), systems of [slavery continued](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_medieval_Europe) in the successor Islamic and Christian kingdoms of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) through the early modern era of the Atlantic slave trade.[^cite_note-33] Arlindo Caldeira writes that in 1444, six caravels belonging to a consortium of seamen and merchants docked in [Lagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos,_Portugal) with a cargo of 235 enslaved men, women, and children who had been captured in the [Bay of Arguin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Arguin) in what is today [Mauritania](18778516.md). These were mostly Muslim Berbers, but also, for the first time, included black-skinned people, likely from sub-Saharan Africa, who had been brought to North Africa over the [Trans-Saharan caravan routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Saharan_slave_trade). Caldeira says "historians recognized this episode as the beginning of the Atlantic slave trade", although ships in Prince Henry the Navigator's fleet had been bringing enslaved persons from West Africa since 1441. The Portuguese established their first trading post as early as 1448 at Arguin, and in 1455 built a fortress to defend the Portuguese merchants settled there.[^cite_note-Caldeira2024-34]



A map of the [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) (red) and [Portuguese Empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire) (blue) in the period of their personal union (1581–1640)

In the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), religion and not race was a determining factor for who was considered to be a legitimate target of slavery. While Christians did not enslave Christians and Muslims did not enslave Muslims, both allowed the enslavement of people they regarded as heretics or insufficient in their religion. This allowed Catholic Christians to enslave Orthodox Christians, and Sunni Muslims to enslave Shia Muslims. Christians and Muslims approved of enslaving [pagans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paganism), who came to be a preferred and comparatively profitable target of the slave trade, though on a much smaller scale than the trade in sub-Saharan Black African slaves.[^cite_note-Korpela2018-35] White slavery was notable throughout the 14th and 15th centuries, and white slaves were one of the largest slave populations. The slaves sold in Spain during medieval times came from the Black Sea area and the Balkans via the [Balkan slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_slave_trade) and the [Black Sea slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_slave_trade).[^cite_note-Casares2014-36]

In the 15th century, the Balkan slave trade was taken over by the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md),[^cite_note-Barker2021-37] and the Black Sea slave trade, supplanted by the [Crimean slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_slave_trade), was closed off from Europe. Spain and Portugal replaced this source of slaves by importing slaves from the conquered [Canary Islands](5717.md) and then from mainland Africa. The mainland trade was initially from [Arab slave traders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_slave_trade), via the [Trans-Saharan slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Saharan_slave_trade) from [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Libya), and then from the African West coast through Portuguese outposts, which developed into the Atlantic slave trade and expanded after the establishment of plantations in the Americas.[^cite_note-Fynn-Paul2021-38]

By this time, Europeans were using both race and religion as a justification to purchase enslaved [sub-Saharan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-Saharan) Africans. An increase of enslaved African people from [Senegal](27278.md) occurred in the Iberian Peninsula in the 15th century. As the number of Senegalese slaves grew larger, Europeans developed terminologies that associated slavery with skin color. The largest numbers of enslaved Africans were held in Andalusian towns, especially [Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville). Colin A. Palmer says, "In 1479 the Spaniards signed the [Treaty of Alcáçovas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Alc%C3%A1%C3%A7ovas) with the Portuguese, granting traders in that country the right to supply Spaniards with enslaved Africans."[^cite_note-Palmer1996-39]



[Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah) curses [Ham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ham_\(Bible\)) by [Gustave Doré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustave_Dore) – the [curse of Ham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curse_of_Ham) was used as a justification to enslave Africans.

[^cite_note-Sweet1997-40]

Europeans enslaved Muslims and people practicing other religions as a justification to Christianise them. In 1452, [Pope Nicholas V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Nicholas_V#slavery) issued the [papal bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_bull) *[Dum Diversas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dum_Diversas)* which gave the King of Portugal the right to enslave non-Christians in perpetual slavery. Thomas says "\[t]his clause was obviously intended to include the natives of West Africa." In 1454, Pope Nicholas issued *[Romanus Pontifex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanus_Pontifex)*,[^cite_note-Thomas2013-41] which laid the foundation for doctrines supportive of empire building.[^cite_note-Lind2020-42]

According to Thomas, *conversos*, former Jews who had been forcibly converted to Christianity,[^cite_note-43] financed much of the slave trade in Lisbon during the 16th and 17th centuries. He writes that one can speculate how far Muslim mullah-merchants controlled the medieval trade in Black Africans across the [Sahara](325363.md) before Portuguese ships were seen in West Africa.[^cite_note-Thomas2013_12-44] In [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain), Spaniards applied the concept of *[limpieza de sangre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limpieza_de_sangre)* (blood purity) to Africans and Native Americans. Believing their ancestries to be impure even though they might be "New Christians", the Spaniards created a racial caste system, with Black blood more often considered a blemish on a person's lineage.[^cite_note-Martínez2004-45]

Ellen Yvonne Simms cites Morner's proposition of three main types of social stratification in colonial New Spain: a caste system, in which membership was set by birth, the estate system, a hierarchy in which rank was determined by customs and laws, and a class system based mainly on economic disparity which allowed upward social mobility. According to Simms, their racist ideology allowed the Spaniards to build a society in colonial New Spain based on racial distinctions, with a white Spanish elite minority controlling all its social, economic, and political aspects, and dominating a large indigenous population alongside the numerous enslaved Blacks who remained at the bottom of the social hierarchy.[^cite_note-Simms2008-46]

### African slavery

Main article: [Slavery in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Africa)

See also: [History of slavery in the Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_the_Muslim_world)

Slavery was prevalent in many parts of Africa for many centuries before the Atlantic slave trade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELovejoy20001-47] Claire C Robertson writes that while chattel systems aimed to sustain a permanent underclass available for cheap labor, African slavery "historically did not conform to a typical chattel slavery model but was highly varied, especially for women." Historians of African slavery have now generally absorbed the Miers and Kopytoff concept of slavery as a continuum of statuses rather than as a monolith, and discarded chattel slavery as the paradigm of all slavery.[^cite_note-Robertson2012-48] Slavery remained a significant part of the African economy after slave exports declined and then collapsed in the second half of the 19th century. Consequently, slaves were used more widely in the African economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELovejoy2000190-49]

Millions of enslaved Africans were transported to other parts of Africa, or exported to Europe and Asia prior to the Atlantic slave trade and the [European colonization of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas).[^cite_note-Clarence-Smith2006-50][^cite_note-Ferro221-51] The [Trans-Saharan slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Saharan_slave_trade) across the Sahara had functioned since antiquity, and continued until the 20th-century. Slaves from eastern Sudan, Somalia, and Ethiopia were marched in shackles to the coast and packed into [dhows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhow) and trafficked [across the Red Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea_slave_trade) or the [Gulf of Aden](58716.md) to markets in Arabia, the [Persian Gulf](24761.md), and the [Indian Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean_slave_trade).[^cite_note-Miers2003-52]

Yves Beigbeder says 5.15 million slaves were sold in Muslim countries from the 16th to the 19th centuries, and cites Roger Botte's estimate that until the 20th century from 12 to 15 million Africans were sold in the Arab slave trade.[^cite_note-Beigbeder2006-53] Raymond Mauny estimates a total of 14 million Black slaves were traded in Muslim countries through the 20th century, including 300,000 for part of the 20th century.[^cite_note-Mauny1970-54]

According to [John K. Thornton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_K._Thornton), Europeans usually bought enslaved people who had been captured in [endemic warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic_warfare) between African states.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998112-7] People living around the [Niger River](21437.md) would be transported from these markets to the coast and sold in European trading ports, in exchange for [muskets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musket) and manufactured goods such as cloth or alcohol.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199845-55] The European demand for slaves provided a new and larger market for the already existing trade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199894-56] While those held as slaves in their region of Africa could hope to escape, those shipped away had little chance of returning to their homeland.[^cite_note-57]

### European activity



[Elmina Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elmina_Castle) on the [Guinea coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinea_\(region\)), present-day [Ghana](12067.md), was built in 1482 by Portuguese traders. Its primary purpose was trading in gold, but when the outward slave trade from the Gold Coast began, it was used in the slave trade. The Gold Coast was a late entrant to the outward slave trade (though there was an inward trade to supply workers in the gold mines).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShumway201125,_33,_47-58]

According to Michael A. Gomez, after Muslim rule in Portugal ended in 1250, labourers became scarce when the Muslim populace fled to [Granada](38264.md) and the supply of prisoners of war ceased. The Portuguese then began to replace their labour force with West Africans they purchased from Muslim traders.[^cite_note-Blackburn1997-59] Portuguese activity in the Atlantic trade can be said to have begun with [Prince Henry the Navigator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Henry_the_Navigator), who initiated exploration further south along the [West African](67393.md) coast. In 1441 [Nuno Tristão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuno_Trist%C3%A3o) and [Antão Gonçalves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%A3o_Gon%C3%A7alves) reached [Cape Blanc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ras_Nouadhibou), where they took prisoner eleven [Tuareg](103054.md) (Berber) men and one Black woman. The captives were first taken to Portugal and then were returned to West Africa and exchanged for gold dust, ten Black persons, and ostrich eggs.[^cite_note-Gomez2005-60]

Upon discovering new lands, European colonisers began to migrate to and settle in lands outside their native continent. Off the coast of Africa, European migrants, under the directions of the [Kingdom of Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Castile), [invaded and colonised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquest_of_the_Canary_Islands) the [Canary Islands](5717.md) during the 15th century, where they converted much of the land to the production of wine and sugar. They captured native Canary Islanders, the [Guanches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanches), to use as slaves on the islands and across the Christian Mediterranean.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199828–29-61]

Historian John Thornton remarks, "the actual motivation for European expansion and for navigational breakthroughs was little more than to exploit the opportunity for immediate profits made by raiding and the seizure or purchase of trade commodities".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199831-62] Using the Canary Islands as a naval base, Europeans moved their activities down the west coast of Africa, performing raids in which slaves would be captured to be sold in the Mediterranean.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199829–31-63] Although initially successful, "it was not long before African naval forces were alerted to the new dangers, and the Portuguese \[raiding] ships began to meet strong and effective resistance", with the crews of several being killed by African sailors, whose boats were better equipped at traversing the [west-central African](539606.md) coasts and river systems.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199837-64] From 1460 to 1500, the removal of Africans increased as Portugal and Spain built forts along the coast of West Africa. The enslaved Africans worked as domestic servants, artisans, and farmers. Other Africans were taken to work the sugar plantations on the Azores, [Madeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeira),[^cite_note-Butel2002-65] and the Canary and [Cape Verde islands](18962637.md). Europeans participated in African enslavement for religious, labour, and profit motives.[^cite_note-Davis2008-66]

Ivana Elbl says that "although slaves were the most common merchandise in the Portuguese-dominated opening period of the seaborne trade between Europe and Africa, relatively little conclusive information is available on their overall numbers." She has attempted to reconstruct annual export volumes for the period 1450–1521 from contemporary records including customs registers of the Portuguese Crown and trade ledgers, and gives an estimated total volume of this early Atlantic Slave Trade as an average of 2,164 slaves shipped per year and a total of 155,800 for the period.[^cite_note-Elbl1997-67]



Established in [Ghana](12067.md) by the [Swedish African Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Africa_Company), [Cape Coast Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Coast_Castle) was built in 1653 as a trading post that later expanded to other European nations.

By 1494, the Portuguese king had entered agreements with the rulers of West African states that allowed trade, enabling the Portuguese to connect with the "well-developed commercial economy in Africa ... without engaging in hostilities".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199838-68] "Peaceful trade became the rule all along the African coast", although there were exceptions when aggression led to violence. For instance, Portuguese traders attempted to conquer the [Bissagos Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bissagos_Islands) in 1535.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199839-69]

Other European nations followed the examples of Spain and Portugal. In 1530, an English merchant from Plymouth, [William Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hawkins_\(died_c._1554\)), visited the [Guinea Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinea_\(region\)) and left with slaves. In 1564, Hawkin's son [John Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hawkins_\(naval_commander\)) sailed to the Guinea Coast; his voyage was supported by [Elizabeth I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_I). He sailed with four ships crewed by only 150 men, and acquired more than 400 slaves on the coast of Sierra Leone, partly by direct capture and partly by purchase from the Portuguese. He left the African coast on 29 January 1565, and reached [Borburata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borburata) in Venezuela on 3 April.[^cite_note-Morgan2004-70]

In 1571, Portugal, supported by the [Kingdom of Kongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kongo), took control of the southwest region of [Angola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Angola) to secure its threatened economic interest in the area. Although Kongo joined a coalition in 1591 to force the Portuguese out, Portugal had secured a foothold that it occupied until the 20th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199840-71] Despite these incidents of violence between African and European forces, many African states ensured trade went on in their own terms, by imposing custom duties on foreign ships. For example, in 1525, the Kongolese King [Afonso I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afonso_I_of_Kongo) seized a French vessel and its crew for illegally trading on his coast.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199839-69] Afonso complained to the king of Portugal that Portuguese slave traders continued to kidnap his people, causing depopulation.

The historian [David Eltis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Eltis) has generated a dataset to draw the broad outlines of the transatlantic slave trade by reconstructing transatlantic links. He submits that if his estimates of the traffic are correct, then more than half of all Portuguese slaving voyages are included. His data indicates the total number of slaves carried to the Americas in Portuguese vessels during the period 1601–1650 as 439,500, derived by adding 252,000 departures for the Spanish Americas and 187,500 for Brazil. This total would be reduced by an estimated 20 percent mortality rate on the shorter South Atlantic route.[^cite_note-Eltis2001-72]



[Nzinga of Ndongo and Matamba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nzinga_of_Ndongo_and_Matamba) fought against the expansion of the Portuguese Empire in a thirty-year war in present-day Angola.

[Nzinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nzinga_of_Ndongo_and_Matamba), who ruled as queen of the [Ambundu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambundu) Kingdoms of [Ndongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ndongo) (1624–1663) and [Matamba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Matamba) (1631–1663) in present-day Angola, fought a war against the Portuguese Empire's expansion. Initially, Nzinga accommodated the Portuguese. She converted to Christianity and repositioned the Ndongo Kingdom as an intermediary in the slave trade, rather than a source for slaves. This provided her with an ally against hostile neighbouring African Kingdoms; however, the Portuguese continued to encroach on her kingdom to expand their slave trade and establish settlements. Nzinga called for an end to the raids, but the Portuguese declared war on Ndongo in 1626. She allowed sanctuary to runaway slaves from Portuguese controlled territory and organised a youth military organization called *kilombo* against them. Within two years, her army was defeated and she went into exile. She later conquered the Kingdom of Matamba and entered into an alliance with the [Dutch West India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_West_India_Company) and former rival African states. With their help, Nzinga was able to reclaim large parts of Ndongo between 1641 and 1647. She continued to fight the Portuguese until a peace treaty was signed in 1656.[^cite_note-metmuseum.org-73]

Historians have long debated the nature of the relationship between African kingdoms and European traders. The Guyanese historian [Walter Rodney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Rodney) (1972) argued it was an unequal relationship, with Africans being forced into a "colonial" trade with the more economically developed Europeans, exchanging raw materials and slaves for manufactured goods. He argued it was this trade dating back to the 16th century that led to Africa being underdeveloped in his time.[^cite_note-Rodney2009-74] This idea of an unequal relationship was contested by John Thornton, who argued that "the Atlantic slave trade was not nearly as critical to the African economy as these scholars believed" and "African manufacturing \[at this period] was more than capable of handling competition from preindustrial Europe".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199844-75] However, Anne Bailey, commenting on Thornton's suggestion that Africans and Europeans were equal partners in the Atlantic slave trade, wrote:

> \[T]o to see Africans as partners implies equal terms and equal influence on the global and intercontinental processes of the trade. This was simply not the case. Africans had great influence on the processes of enslavement on the continent itself, but they had no direct influence on the engines behind the trade in the capital firms, the shipping and insurance companies of Europe and America, or on the plantation system in the Americas. Likewise, they did not wield any influence on the budding manufacturing centers of the West.[^cite_note-Bailey2005-76]

## 16th, 17th, and 18th centuries



Map of Meridian Line set under the Treaty of Tordesillas



*The Slave Trade* by [Auguste François Biard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_Fran%C3%A7ois_Biard), 1840

The first enslaved Africans arrived in continental North America in the 16th century, often with European explorers. They continued to arrive in limited numbers, not only from Africa but also from Europe, the Caribbean islands, and other Atlantic coastal regions.[^cite_note-Berlin2025-77] During the first Atlantic slave trade system, most of the slavers were Portuguese, giving Portugal a near-monopoly. The [Treaty of Tordesillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tordesillas) did not allow Spanish ships in African ports. Spain relied on Portuguese ships and sailors to bring slaves across the Atlantic. From 1525, slaves were transported directly from the Portuguese colony of [Sao Tomé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sao_Tom%C3%A9) across the Atlantic to [Hispaniola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniola).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoruckiEltisWheat2015446,_457,_460-78] A burial ground in [Campeche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campeche_City), Mexico, suggests enslaved Africans had been brought there not long after [Hernán Cortés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hern%C3%A1n_Cort%C3%A9s) completed the conquest of [Aztec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztecs) and [Mayan](18449273.md) Mexico in 1519. The graveyard was in use from 1550 to the late 17th century.[^cite_note-79] According to Matthew Restall, by the late 1530s there were probably a thousand Blacks in greater Peru, and possibly twice that number by the mid-1540s. Despite the fact that the Spanish civil wars of the 1540s may have slowed imports of slaves and the emigration of Black auxiliaries from Panama, there were some 3,000 Blacks in Peru by the 1550s.[^cite_note-Restall2000-80]

In 1562, [John Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hawkins_\(naval_commander\)) initiated the English slave trade when he sailed as far south on the Guinea coast as Sierra Leone, and according to [Hakluyt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Hakluyt) captured 300 Africans that he transported across the Atlantic to Hispaniola. In 1564, he repeated the voyage, this time using one of the largest ships in Queen Elizabeth's navy, the dry rot-riddled [Jesus of Lübeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_of_L%C3%BCbeck), and realised a good profit. He sailed on a third voyage in 1567, which, although it ended in disaster with only 15 or so survivors among a crew of 408, was a financial success, with nearly all its treasure of gold, silver, and pearls arriving safely in Plymouth.[^cite_note-Morgan2007_online-81]

Around 1560, the Portuguese began a slave trade to Brazil. From 1580 until 1640, Portugal was temporarily united with Spain in the [Iberian Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Union). Most Portuguese contractors who obtained the [asiento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiento_de_Negros) between 1580 and 1640 were [conversos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Converso).[^cite_note-Israel2002-82] For Portuguese merchants, many of whom were "[New Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Christians)" or their descendants, the union of crowns presented commercial opportunities in the slave trade to Spanish America.[^cite_note-LockhartSchwartz1983-83] Portuguese New Christians increasingly allied themselves with the Jewish merchants who dominated the slave trade in [Dutch Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Brazil).[^cite_note-Wiznitzer1960-84]



A [slave market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_market) in Brazil

Until the middle of the 17th century, Mexico was the largest market for slaves in Spanish America.[^cite_note-RawleyBehrendt2005-85] While the Portuguese were directly involved in trading enslaved people to Brazil, the Spanish Empire relied on the [Asiento de Negros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiento_de_Negros) system, awarding [Genoese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa) merchant bankers the license to trade slaves from Africa to their colonies in [Spanish America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_America). [Cartagena](63927.md), [Veracruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veracruz_\(city\)), [Buenos Aires](255919.md), and [Hispaniola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniola) received most slave arrivals, mainly from [Angola](701.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoruckiEltisWheat2015437,_446-86] This division of the slave trade between Spain and Portugal upset the British and Dutch who invested in the [British West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_West_Indies) and [Dutch Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Brazil) producing sugar. After the Iberian Union fell apart, Spain prohibited Portugal from directly engaging in the slave trade as a carrier. Despite the ban, however, the Portuguese continued to indirectly supply the Spanish American slave trade.[^cite_note-García-Montón2017-87]

From 1641 to 1789, Spanish transatlantic traffic was operating at trivial levels. In many years, not a single Spanish slave voyage set sail from Africa. The slave trade was opened to the traditional enemies of Spain, causing Spain to lose a large share to the Dutch, French, and English. Unlike all their imperial competitors, the Spanish almost never delivered slaves to foreign territories. Over the course of the Spanish transatlantic slave trade, Spanish slave ships disembarked more than one million captive Africans in the Americas, with Spanish ships transporting four times more Africans than did their counterparts in the US.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoruckiEltisWheat2015452–454-88]

In 1672, the [Royal Africa Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Africa_Company) was founded. In 1674, the [New West India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_West_India_Company#New_West_India_Company) became more deeply involved in the slave trade.[^cite_note-89] From 1677 the [Compagnie du Sénégal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compagnie_du_S%C3%A9n%C3%A9gal) used [Gorée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gor%C3%A9e) to [house the slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Slaves). That same year the Spanish proposed to get slaves directly from the Dutch West India Company's posts off the coast of Africa, but this was against the [Dutch West India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_West_India_Company) Charter (1621).[^cite_note-Postma2008-90] The Royal African Company usually refused to deliver slaves to Spanish colonies, though they did sell them to all comers from their factories in [Kingston, Jamaica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston,_Jamaica) and [Bridgetown, Barbados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridgetown,_Barbados).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoruckiEltisWheat2015451-91] In 1682, Spain allowed governors from Havana, [Porto Bello, Panama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto_Bello,_Panama), and [Cartagena, Colombia](63927.md) to procure slaves from Jamaica.[^cite_note-RawleyBehrendt2005-85]



Island of [Gorée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gor%C3%A9e), Senegal

By the 1690s, the English were shipping the most slaves from Guinea.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHairLaw1998257-92] By the 18th century, Portuguese Angola again become one of the principal sources of the slave trade.[^cite_note-Domingues_da_Silva2013-93] After the [War of the Spanish Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Spanish_Succession), as part of the provisions of the [Treaty of Utrecht (1713)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Utrecht_\(1713\)), the Asiento was granted to the [South Sea Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sea_Company).[^cite_note-Paul-94] Despite the [South Sea Bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sea_Bubble), the British maintained this position during the 18th century, becoming the biggest shipper of slaves across the Atlantic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChristopher20066-95][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeredith2014191-96] Most of the slave trade took place during the 18th century, with the Portuguese, British, and French being the main carriers of nine out of ten slaves abducted in Africa.[^cite_note-Bradley2011-97] By the mid-18th century, French [Saint-Domingue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Domingue) was the predominant island in the Caribbean. It produced the most sugar of any colony in the Americas, and held the largest slave population in the West Indies.[^cite_note-Klein210_32-98] Slave trading was regarded as crucial to Europe's maritime economy, as noted by an English slave trader: "What a glorious and advantageous trade this is ... It is the hinge on which all the trade of this globe moves."[^cite_note-Wheeler2010-99][^cite_note-Houstoun1747-100]

Slave trading became a business for [privately owned enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privately_held_company#Privately_owned_enterprise), reducing international complications.[^cite_note-RawleyBehrendt2005-85] After 1790, captains often compared slave prices in major markets of Kingston, Havana, and [Charleston, South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina) before deciding where to sell.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoruckiEltisWheat2015439-101] For the last sixteen years of the transatlantic slave trade, Spain was the only transatlantic slave-trading empire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoruckiEltisWheat2015457-102] Following the British [Slave Trade Act 1807](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_Trade_Act_1807) and [U.S. bans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_Prohibiting_Importation_of_Slaves) on the African slave trade, it declined, but the period thereafter still accounted for a substantial portion of the Atlantic slave trade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELovejoy2000145-103] Between 1810 and 1860, over 3.5 million slaves were transported, with 850,000 in the 1820s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeredith2014193-104]

### Triangular trade

Main article: [Triangular trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangular_trade)



Diagram of [triangular trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangular_trade)

The first side of the triangle was the export of goods from Europe to Africa. African kings and merchants took part in the trading of slaves from 1440 to about 1833. For each captive, the African rulers would receive goods from Europe. These included guns, gunpowder, alcohol,[^cite_note-Alpern1995-105] [indigo-dyed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigo_dye#History) Indian textiles,[^cite_note-Kobayashi2019-106] and other factory-made goods.[^cite_note-Alpern1995-105] The second leg of the triangle, the so-called "Middle Passage",[^cite_note-EltisRichardsonMisevich2026-107] exported enslaved Africans across the Atlantic to the Americas and Caribbean Islands. The third part of the triangle was the return of goods to Europe from the Americas. The goods were the products of [slave plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_plantation) and included cotton, sugar, tobacco, coffee,[^cite_note-Kobayashi2019-106] [molasses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molasses), and rum.[^cite_note-Bailey1992-108] Sir [John Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hawkins_\(naval_commander\)), often considered the pioneer of the English slave trade, was the first to run the triangular trade route.[^cite_note-BakerLambert2024-109]

Some historians, such as Sophie Campbell, argue that there is over-emphasis on the importance of any triangular trade.[^cite_note-Campbell2023-110] Gilman Ostrander denied it was a major factor in colonial commerce and called it a myth.[^cite_note-Ostrander2019-111] Whilst it was common initially, within 50 years, two-leg voyages to Africa from the Americas started to make up a significant proportion of the whole of the transatlantic slave trade. Though that share fluctuated over time, it is likely that ships based in the New World carried a larger total of enslaved people across the Atlantic from Africa, compared to the numbers moved in ships with a home port in Europe. Ships from the three largest slaving ports in South America ([Rio de Janeiro](25936.md), [Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador,_Bahia) and [Recife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recife)) transported many more captives from Africa than the total of those from the four major European slaving ports ([London](17867.md), [Liverpool](18081.md), [Bristol](36741.md) and [Nantes](67453.md)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEltis202547,_58-112][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAraujo202481–82-113]

David Eltis emphasises the importance of the two systems of wind and ocean currents in the North and South Atlantic Ocean that determined how the transatlantic slave trade was physically carried out. One system lies north of the equator and turns clockwise, while the other lies south of the equator and turns counterclockwise. The northern "wheel" connected Western Europe and West Africa with the Caribbean and North America and was dominated by the English, while the southern "wheel" linked West-Central Africa (mainly Angola) directly to Brazil, an enormous traffic controlled by the Portuguese, who for three hundred years carried on the slave trade with the largest volume.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEltis2007-3] [Pieter Emmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piet_Emmer) says there were two Atlantic systems operative in the first phase of the outward expansion of European commercial activity, including the slave trade, during the [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period). The first one was created by the Portuguese and the Spanish and the second one by the British, the French, and the Dutch.[^cite_note-Emmer1991-114]

### Labour and slavery



A [Wedgwood anti-slavery medallion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wedgwood_anti-slavery_medallion), produced in 1787 by [Josiah Wedgwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josiah_Wedgwood)

[^cite_note-Guyatt2000-115]

The Atlantic slave trade was the result, in part, of a [labour shortage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_shortage) created by the desire of colonists to exploit New World land and resources for profit. [Native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) peoples were exploited as slave labour by Europeans until a large number died from overwork, [Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World) diseases, and maltreatment, conditions which also led to low fertility among them.[^cite_note-O'Brien2017-116] In the mid-16th century, the Spanish [New Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Laws) prohibited enslavement of the Indigenous people, causing a labour shortage. Alternative sources of labour, such as [indentured servitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indentured_servitude), failed to provide a sufficient workforce. Many crops could not be sold for profit, or even grown, in Europe. Exporting crops and goods from the New World to Europe often proved to be more profitable than producing them in Europe. A vast amount of labour was needed to create and sustain plantations that required intensive labour to grow, harvest, and process prized tropical crops. When the enslavement of Indigenous peoples failed to meet their labour demands, the Portuguese and Spanish shifted to the importation of African slaves, followed later by the English and the French. This transition began around the mid-16th century in Iberia, and included Brazil as well; it occurred approximately between 1650 and 1750 in the Dutch, British, and French colonies.[^cite_note-Zeuske2023-117] West Africa (part of which became known as "the [Slave Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_Coast_of_West_Africa)"), Angola and nearby Kingdoms and later [Central Africa](539606.md), became the primary source of enslaved people to meet the increased demand.[^cite_note-Dowlah2024-118]

[Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) attributed the use of slave labour in part to the climate, and the consequent idle leisure afforded by slave labour: "For in a warm climate, no man will labour for himself who can make another labour for him. This is so true, that of the proprietors of slaves a very small proportion indeed are ever seen to labour."[^cite_note-Jefferson2007-119] The economist Elena Esposito argues that the enslavement of Africans in America was attributable to the fact that the American south was sufficiently warm and humid for malaria to thrive; the disease had debilitating effects on the European settlers. Conversely, many enslaved Africans were taken from regions of Africa which hosted potent strains of the disease, so they had developed resistance. This resulted in higher malaria survival rates in the American south among enslaved Africans than European labourers, making them a more profitable source of labour and encouraging their use.[^cite_note-120]

David Eltis argues that Africans were enslaved by Europeans because of cultural beliefs that prohibited the enslavement of group insiders, even if there was another source of labour available to be enslaved, such as convicts, prisoners of war or vagrants. He argues that traditional beliefs existed in Europe against enslaving Christians, while those subject to enslavement were non-Christians or natives of Africa and their immediate descendants (a slave converting to Christianity did not guarantee emancipation). Eltis also says that while all slave societies have demarked insiders and outsiders, Europeans took this process further by extending the status of insider to the entire European continent, rendering it unthinkable to enslave a European since this would require enslaving an insider. Africans were viewed as outsiders and thus qualified for enslavement.[^cite_note-Eltis2000-121] According to the historian Anita Rupprecht, British insurance underwriters were never able to settle the question of whether slaves were commodities or human beings, but Africans always refused to be treated as goods. They "struggled, rebelled, mounted insurrections, mutinied, took over ships and embraced \[...] piracy." Cases like the [Zong massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zong_massacre), in which the captain of the slave ship *Zong* ordered 133 Africans thrown overboard, occurred without justice for the victims. Granville Sharp's narrative of the incident was widely publicised, providing effective propaganda for the British abolitionist cause.[^cite_note-Rupprecht2007-122]

### African participation



Slave traders in [Gorée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gor%C3%A9e), Senegal, 18th century

Africans, including rulers, traders and military aristocrats, played a direct role in the slave trade. They sold slaves acquired from wars to increase their personal wealth and thus their power. Per Thornton, there was a very large preexisting slave population in Africa when the first Europeans arrived and during the entire period of the slave trade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998107–109,_294-123] Those sold into slavery were usually from a different ethnic group than those who captured them, whether enemies or just neighbours. These captive slaves were considered "other", not part of the people of the ethnic group or "tribe".[^cite_note-MiersKopytoff1977-124]

The kingdom of Dahomey supplied war captives to European slave traders. The Dahomey King [Agaja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agaja), who ruled from 1708 to 1732,[^cite_note-Falola2007-125] took control of key trade routes for the Atlantic slave trade by conquering the neighbouring kingdoms of [Allada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allada) in 1724 and [Whydah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Whydah) in 1727.[^cite_note-Law1989-126] According to Falola and Warnock, control of this coast brought Dahomey into contact with the Dutch, the French, and the English, following which slavery became a state monopoly and the "cornerstone of the Dahomean economy", with extensive plantations maintained by slave labour.[^cite_note-Falola2007-125]

Africans from the Gold Coast (present-day Ghana) participated in the slave trade through intermarriage, or *[cassare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassare)*, meaning 'to set up house', derived from the Portuguese *'casar'*, meaning 'to marry'. *Cassare* formed political and economic bonds between European and African slave traders. *Cassare* was a pre-European-contact practice used to integrate the "other" from a differing African tribe. Early on in the Atlantic slave trade, it was common for elite West African families to marry their women to European traders in alliance, bolstering their syndicate. The marriages were even performed using African customs, which Europeans did not object to, given how important the connections were.[^cite_note-Ipsen2015-127]

### African awareness of the conditions of slavery in the Americas



[William Ansah Sessarakoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Ansah_Sessarakoo), the son of Eno Baisie Kurentsi (John Currantee) of [Anomabu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anomabu). Both father and son were active slave traders, with John Currantee being the political leader of this major slave exporting town.

[^cite_note-128]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShumway201175,_80-129]

It is difficult to reconstruct and generalise how local people in Africa understood the Atlantic slave trade during the period, though according to Robin Law, it seems clear that in Ouidah, and among the ruling elites in Dahomey, there was an informed understanding of where slaves were transported to, and happened to them when they got there.[^cite_note-Law2004-130][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESparks2014243-131] According to Robin Law, the elites of [Dahomey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahomey) must have had an "informed understanding" of the fates of the Africans they sold into slavery. Dahomey sent diplomats to Brazil and Portugal who returned with information about the trips. Royal elites of Dahomey had experienced slavery in the Americas before returning to their homeland. The only apparent moral issue that the kingdom had with slavery was the enslavement of fellow Dahomeyans, an offense punishable by death, rather than the institution of slavery itself.[^cite_note-Law2004-130]

On the Gold Coast, it was common for slave-trading African rulers to encourage their children to learn about Europeans by sending them to sail on European ships, live inside European forts, or travel to Europe or America for an education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESparks2014186–197-132] African diplomats traveled to European cities. Families rescued members tricked into slavery in the Americas by sending demands to the Dutch and British governments, who complied due to fears of reduced trade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESparks2014186–197-132] An example is the case of [William Ansah Sessarakoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Ansah_Sessarakoo), the son of a Fante slave trader on the Gold Coast, who was sold into slavery in Barbados by the British ship's captain who was supposed to take him to England to be educated. His father, a *caboceer*, or powerful political leader, was able to shut down the British trade until he was emancipated by the [Royal African Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_African_Company).[^cite_note-Hanley2015-133] [Fenda Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenda_Lawrence) was a slave trader from [the Gambia](65656.md) who traveled and traded in [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_\(U.S._State\)) and [South Carolina](27956.md) as a free person before returning home.[^cite_note-Akyeampong2012-134]

A common assumption by Africans who were unaware of the purpose of the Atlantic slave trade was that they had been sold to cannibals who planned on cooking and eating their captives. This belief was a source of significant distress for enslaved Africans; Thornton says it was a way of expressing their feeling that they considered themselves "the victims of a plot involving greedy and selfish people."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998316-135]

### African opposition to the slave trade



[Boukary Koutou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wobgho)'s [Mossi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mossi_people) cavalry returning with captives from a raid, [Ouagadougou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ouagadougou)



Burning of a village in Africa, and capture of its inhabitants. To escape slave raids some Africans fled into swamp regions or to other areas.

While African merchants maintained control over the slave trade in Africa,[^cite_note-Richardson2003-136] some African rulers organised military resistance movements and fought African slave raiders and European slave traders entering their villages.



On July 1, 1839, enslaved [Mende](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mende_people) people aboard the [*Amistad*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Amistad) revolted and took control of the ship. This incident led to a [Supreme Court case in 1841](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._The_Amistad).

[^cite_note-137]

African resistance occurred in every phase of the slave trade—Africans resisted forced marches to the slave holding stations, they resisted at the coast, and they were prone to revolt on the slave ships. In 1729 the enslaved Africans aboard the slave ship *Clare* seized its firearms and gunpowder, drove the crew from the vessel, and landed the ship near [Cape Coast Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Coast_Castle) in present-day [Ghana](12067.md).[^cite_note-Wax1966-138] Enslaved Africans sank ships, killed their crews, and set fire to other ships with explosives. Investors in slaving voyages saw to it that precautions were taken to prevent shipboard rebellions by such means as separating the men from women and children in the [hold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hold_\(compartment\)) of the ship. Men were frequently shackled while the ships were anchored near the African coast, and all the captives were watched round the clock by armed crewmen.[^cite_note-Richardson2024-139]

Shipping records indicate that from 1698 to 1807 there were 350 acts of insurrection aboard slave ships. According to Richardson, perhaps as many as one of every ten transatlantic slaving voyages experienced an insurrection; most of them were defeated. Ship owners invested in cannon, [swivel guns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swivel_gun), firearms, and extra crew to prevent escapes or revolts.[^cite_note-Richardson2003-136] To thwart suicides, crewmen placed nets around slave ships to catch enslaved persons who jumped overboard. An instrument called the "*speculum oris*" was used to force feed those who tried to starve themselves.[^cite_note-Snyder2010-140] There was a common stereotype that the [Igbo people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people) were unusually prone to commit suicide.[^cite_note-Richardson2003-136] Snyder says although planters in South Carolina and slave traders in the West Indies avoided the Igbo because of reports that they were prone to suicide, 38 percent of all slaves imported to Virginia between 1710 and 1760 were Igbo.[^cite_note-141]

[John Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Newton) was the captain of an English slave ship who later became an ordained minister and an abolitionist. He recorded in his [ship's log](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ship's_log) several attempted insurrections on his own ship, and observes that some were successful in seizing other ships.[^cite_note-Forbes1992-142] In 1730 the slave ship *Little George* departed from the [Guinea Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinea_\(region\)) and sailed for [Rhode Island](25410.md) with a cargo of 96 enslaved Africans. A few slaves slipped out of their shackles, killed three watchmen on deck, and imprisoned the captain and crew. The revolting slaves took control of the ship and sailed it back to the [Sierra Leone River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_Leone_River) in Africa. After failing to frighten them with a bottle bomb and threats of sinking the ship, the captain and his crew made a deal with the Africans for the opposing sides to liberate each other. When the ship reached the shoreline, the Africans fled.[^cite_note-143] According to research by [Jane Landers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane_Landers), more rebellions occurred when there were large numbers of African women aboard.[^cite_note-Landers2013-144]

### European participation

Because there were no healthier highlands or inland regions in West Africa where European settlers could have found a sanctuary, they mostly had to confine themselves to the immediate coast where they were exposed to rampant [tropical diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_disease). The high risk of dying because of their lack of immunity to malaria and yellow fever led to the area being known as the "white man's grave".[^cite_note-Curtin-145] The cost of high mortality rates was far too high to justify establishing a permanent population, while coastal trade required a comparatively limited presence.[^cite_note-ÖbergRönnbäck2016-146] Europeans typically resided in fortresses on the coasts, where they waited for Africans to provide them captured slaves from the interior in exchange for goods. The historian [Ana Lucia Araujo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ana_Lucia_Araujo) gives the example of the three kingdoms of the Loango coast in West Central Africa, Loango, [Kakongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakongo), and Ngoyo, who, recognizing the need to maintain their sovereignty to carry on free trade, kept control over "their territories and never allowed any European power to establish permanent trading posts such as castles, forts, and fortresses in the region during the era of the Atlantic slave trade."[^cite_note-Araujo2025-147]

Cases of European merchants kidnapping free Africans into slavery often resulted in retaliation from Africans, who would stop the slave trade and capture or kill Europeans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESparks201446-148] Europeans who desired uninterrupted trade aimed to prevent kidnapping incidents, and the British passed the "Acts of Parliament for Regulating the Slave Trade" in 1750 which outlawed the abduction of free Africans by "fraud, force, or violence".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESparks201446-148] According to the Lowcountry Digital Library, "When Portuguese, and later their European competitors, found that peaceful commercial relations alone did not generate enough enslaved Africans to fill the growing demands of the trans-Atlantic slave trade, they formed military alliances with certain African groups against their enemies. This encouraged more extensive warfare to produce captives for trading."[^cite_note-149]



An enslaved African man being inspected for sale

As Britain rose in naval power and settled continental North America and some islands of the [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies), they became the leading slave traders.[^cite_note-150] At one stage the trade was the monopoly of the [Royal African Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_African_Company), operating out of London. But, following the loss of the company's monopoly in 1698,[^cite_note-Elkins1976-151] [Bristol](36741.md) and [Liverpool](18081.md) merchants became increasingly involved in the trade.[^cite_note-Rawley2003-152] The historian [Kenneth Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_O._Morgan) writes that during the entire period of the British slave trade Liverpool [slave ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_ship) delivered more than an estimated 1,171,000 slaves to the New World, making it the foremost port of origin of transatlantic slaving voyages before the 19th century.[^cite_note-Morgan2007-153] Much of the wealth on which the city of [Manchester](20206.md), and surrounding towns, was built in the late 18th century, and for much of the 19th century, was based on the processing of slave-picked cotton and manufacture of cloth.[^cite_note-154] Other British cities also profited from the slave trade. [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham), the largest [gun-producing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_Quarter,_Birmingham) town in Britain at the time, supplied guns to be traded for slaves.[^cite_note-155] London was always the preeminent British port in the sugar trade, and it accounted for about three-quarters of English sugar imports before 1776. Morgan says "cane sugar from the Caribbean was the most valuable British import in the century and a half down to 1820" and that per capita sugar consumption rose in England from 1 lb. to 25 lbs. between 1670 and 1770.[^cite_note-Morgan1993-156]

In 1778, [Thomas Kitchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Kitchin) estimated that Europeans were bringing an estimated 52,000 slaves to the Caribbean yearly, with the French bringing the most Africans to the [French West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_West_Indies).[^cite_note-Kitchin1778-157] The Atlantic slave trade peaked in the last two decades of the 18th century,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998304-158] during and following the [Kongo Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kongo_Civil_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998305-159] Wars among tiny states along the Niger River's [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people)-inhabited region and the accompanying banditry spiked in this period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998310-160] Another reason for the peak was major warfare conducted by expanding states, such as the [kingdom of Dahomey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Dahomey),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998311-161] the [Oyo Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oyo_Empire), and the [Ashanti Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashanti_Empire).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton1998122-162]

### Slave market regions and participation



Major slave trading regions of Africa, 15th–19th centuries

Europeans would buy and ship slaves to the Western Hemisphere from markets across West Africa. The number of enslaved people sold to the New World varied throughout the slave trade. As for the distribution of slaves from regions of activity, certain areas produced far more enslaved people than others. According to Paul E. Lovejoy, "West-central Africa, including the coast from Cabinda and Loango to Angola, accounted for the majority of slaves exported to the Americas until the last quarter of the seventeenth century and a major share of the total thereafter."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELovejoy200053-163]

### Ethnic groups

According to the historian [Gwendolyn Midlo Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwendolyn_Midlo_Hall), some scholars have proposed and defend the hypothesis that the supposed ethnicities of Africans is a [social construct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_construct) dating from the end of the 19th century. She writes that "European missionaries, anthropologists, and administrators invented African ethnicities in order to create conflicts among the peoples they ruled". Further, she says that historically, distinctive ethnicities were less developed in West Central Africa than in some other West African regions, and that the terms "Angola" and "Kongo" were used widely by Europeans for many diverse peoples. Hall cites [Joseph C. Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_C._Miller) as calling for "more contextualised studies by historians who posit ethnic continuities among Africans brought to the Americas."[^cite_note-Hall2006-164]

## Human toll



Slave trade out of Africa, 1500–1900

&#x20;The trade led to the destruction of individuals and cultures. The historian [Ana Lucia Araujo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ana_Lucia_Araujo) says:

> The process of enslavement did not end with arrival on the American shores; in this context, the different paths taken by the individuals and groups who were victims of the Atlantic slave trade were influenced by a myriad of factors including the disembarking region, the kind of work performed, gender, age, religion, and language. These elements very often oriented the paths toward freedom available to these men and women.[^cite_note-Araujo2011-165]

[Patrick Manning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Manning_\(historian\)) estimates that about 12 million slaves entered the Atlantic trade between the 16th and 19th centuries, but about 1.5 million died on board ship. About 10.5 million slaves arrived in the Americas. Besides the slaves who died on the Middle Passage, more Africans likely died during the slave raids and wars in Africa and forced marches to ports. Manning estimates that 4 million died inside Africa after capture, and many more died young. His estimate includes the 12 million who were originally destined for the Atlantic, as well as the 6 million destined for Arabian slave markets and the 8 million destined for African markets.[^cite_note-Manning1992-17] According to Angus Madden, in the 17th century the Dutch, French, and British introduced plantation agriculture to the Caribbean islands seized from Spain. After their indigenous populations had been mostly wiped out, 3.8 million slaves were transported to the Caribbean islands during the colonial period. Between 1500 and 1870, the same number were carried to Brazil.[^cite_note-Maddison2007-166]

### Destinations and flags of carriers

Most of the Atlantic slave trade was carried out by seven nations and most of the slaves were carried to their own colonies in the new world. But there was also significant other trading which is shown in the table below.[^cite_note-167] The records are not complete, and some data is uncertain. The last rows show that there were also smaller numbers of slaves carried to Europe and to other parts of Africa, and at least 1.8 million did not survive the journey and were buried at sea with little ceremony.

### African conflicts



Diagram of a large slave ship. [Thomas Clarkson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Clarkson): *The cries of Africa to the inhabitants of Europe*, c. 1822

J.C. Sharman writes that enormous growth in the demand for slaves gave African rulers more opportunities for state building. Many of them developed "highly militarized predatory slaving states" that first appeared in West Africa as a consequence of the expansion of the transatlantic slave trade in the late 17th century. These rulers used the increased returns from predatory aggression to build armies and states by conscripting slaves as soldiers and trading for guns. The demand for enslaved persons remained high even after the Atlantic slave trade was abolished, with the number of enslaved Africans reaching its highest point near the end of the 19th century.[^cite_note-Sharman2023-168] The presence of European slavers affected the way in which the legal code in African societies responded to offenders. Crimes traditionally punishable by some other form of punishment became punishable by enslavement and sale to slave traders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELovejoy200089–90-169]

According to Patrick Manning, warfare was the principal means by which slaves were captured. He cites as an example the constant wars in the [Bight of Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bight_of_Benin) during the late 17th and early 18th centuries, occurring when slave exports from that region were greatest. He refers to John Thornton's work in recovering descriptions of wars between armies of several thousand in 17th-century Angola, with baggage trains made up mostly of women within each army—slave traders would wait nearby to purchase the defeated combatants, a "preselected population" ready to be exported, from the winners.[^cite_note-Manning1990-170] Thornton identifies two aspects of African societies that inclined them to engage in the slave trade: use of slaves in the domestic economy, especially to raise revenue for consolidating a state, and in warfare. He cites Philip Curtin's analysis of the Senegambian slave trade of the 18th century, from which Curtin derived his view that African warfare leading to slave captures was due to either economic or political factors, although wars might be fought for mixed reasons. He infers that the data support the political model.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton199899–100-171]

Michael A. Gomez says that deaths of captives from initial capture to the barracoons on the coast averaged 10 to 15 per cent, reaching as high as 40 per cent in Angola,[^cite_note-Gomez1998-172] while Nathan Nunn says that death rates on the forced marches from inland areas to slave ports on the various coasts are estimated to have ranged from 10 per cent to 50 per cent.[^cite_note-Nunn2008-173]

Before the arrival of the [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people), slavery had already existed in the [Kingdom of Kongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kongo). [Afonso I of Kongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afonso_I_of_Kongo) believed that the slave trade should be subject to Kongo law. When he suspected the Portuguese of receiving illegally enslaved persons to sell, he wrote to King João III in 1526 imploring him to put a stop to the practice.[^cite_note-174]

[Dahomey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahomey) developed as a warrior-state with an economy that became dependent on the transatlantic slave trade. Its kings sold [war captives](25008.md) into transatlantic slavery as their main source of revenue, a trade which was entwined with the religious practise of human sacrifice at the [Annual Customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_annual_customs_of_Dahomey), the kingdom's primary public ceremony.[^cite_note-Law1997-175] King Gezo was quoted in The Anglo-American Magazine as saying in 1849:

> The slave-trade has been the ruling principle of my people. It is the source of their glory and wealth. Their songs celebrate their victories, and the mother lulls the child to sleep with notes of triumph over an enemy reduced to slavery. Can I by signing such a treaty, change the sentiments of a whole people? It cannot be\![^cite_note-Anglo-American1854-176]

&#x20;The [Empire of Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_Empire) grew increasingly rich during the 16th and 17th centuries on the slave trade with Europe; slaves from enemy states of the interior were sold and carried to the Americas in Dutch and Portuguese ships. The Bight of Benin's shore soon came to be known as the "Slave Coast". Like the [Bambara Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bambara_Empire) to the east, the [Khasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khasso) kingdoms depended heavily on the slave trade for their economy. A family's status was indicated by the number of slaves it owned, leading to wars for the sole purpose of taking more captives. This trade led the Khasso into increasing contact with the [European](9239.md) settlements of Africa's west coast, particularly the French.[^cite_note-177] In 1807, the [Parliament of the United Kingdom](13964.md) passed the bill that abolished the trading of slaves. According to the historian Kenneth Onwuka Dike, the European abolition movement was puzzling to contemporary Africans, as the English, who had been preeminent in the slave trade, suddenly began opposing it zealously. The trade had been the foundation of the economies of the slave-trading African kingdoms including Dahomey, Bonny, Lagos, and many others that owed their existence and power to the rise of the slave trade. The king of Bonny (now in [Nigeria](21383.md)), the predominant slave market in Africa, declared in 1807 (paraphrased):

> We \[i.e. the king and Council] think that this trade must go on. That also is the verdict of our Oracle and the priests. They say that your country, however great, can never stop a trade ordained by God himself.[^cite_note-Dike1966-178]

### Atlantic shipment



The French slave ship *Marie-Séraphique* carried an average of 357 enslaved persons during its six voyages to Africa.

After being captured and held in the factories, enslaved persons were shipped on the infamous [Middle Passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Passage) across the Atlantic Ocean, on which staggering numbers of them died.[^cite_note-Meltzer1993-179] Their deaths were the result of brutal treatment and poor care from the time of their capture and throughout their voyage, on which they were packed into tight, unsanitary spaces aboard ships for months at a time.[^cite_note-Duquette2014-180] Measures were taken to stem the onboard mortality rate, such as enforced "dancing" (as exercise) above deck and the practice of force-feeding enslaved persons who tried to starve themselves.[^cite_note-Snyder2010-140] The conditions on board also resulted in the spread of fatal diseases. Other fatalities were suicides, slaves who escaped by jumping overboard.[^cite_note-Snyder2010-140]

Radburn and Eltis describe how the famous diagram of the Liverpool slave ship [*Brooks*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooks_\(1781_ship\)) published in January 1789 by the [Society for Effecting the Abolition of the Slave Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_for_Effecting_the_Abolition_of_the_Slave_Trade) has come to symbolise the horrors of the captive African experience of the Middle Passage. The society disseminated thousands of copies of the image in posters, newspapers, pamphlets, magazines, and books. The scholars say the French ship *Marie-Séraphique* better represents typical British and French slave ships in the second half of the 18th century than does *Brooks*. *Marie-Séraphique* carried an average of 357 enslaved persons during its six voyages to Africa, while British vessels of the period usually carried about 267 captives and French slavers carried about 325. For comparison, the unusually large *Brooks* carried more than double the average of British vessels.[^cite_note-RadburnEltis2019-181]

Raymond L. Cohn, an economics professor whose research has focused on [economic history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history) and [international migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_migration),[^cite_note-182] has researched the [mortality rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_rate) among Africans during the voyages of the Atlantic slave trade. He found that mortality rates decreased over the history of the slave trade, primarily because the length of time necessary for the voyage was declining. "In the eighteenth century many slave voyages took at least 2½ months. In the nineteenth century, 2 months appears to have been the maximum length of the voyage, and many voyages were far shorter. Fewer slaves died in the Middle Passage over time mainly because the passage was shorter."[^cite_note-183]

The ordinary sailors on [slave ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_ship) were badly paid and subject to harsh discipline. Mortality of around 20%, a number similar and sometimes greater than those of the slaves,[^cite_note-Curtin1969_282-184] was expected in a ship's crew during the course of a voyage; this was due to disease, flogging, overwork, or slave uprisings.[^cite_note-Edwards(Captain.)2007-185] Disease ([malaria](20423.md) or [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever)) was the most common cause of death among sailors. A high crew mortality rate on the return voyage was in the captain's interests as it reduced the number of sailors who had to be paid on reaching the home port.[^cite_note-186]
